I just find myself eating. \n\nAlthough, you know, having her on my mind right now... I was actually just looking at this old photograph of her on the mantelpiece yesterday. She always had this old Kodak camera with her. The kind with those little flash cubes that would spin around and pop? She took pictures of absolutely everything. Every holiday, every bird that landed on the birdbath. We used to take the little plastic canisters of film down to the pharmacy on Main Street to get them developed. Mr. Gable ran the place back then. Tall, gangly fellow, always wore a bow tie and suspenders. He kept a big glass jar of those butterscotch candies on the counter, the ones wrapped in the yellow cellophane. I haven't seen those candies in years. I wonder if they even make them anymore? They used to get terribly stuck in my back molars. Dental work is just outrageously expensive nowadays, anyway. My dentist, Dr. Evans, he actually just retired last month and bought an RV to drive down to Florida. Can you imagine driving a vehicle that big on the interstate? \n\nBut anyway, I'm sorry. To answer your question again, no, I don't think I'm eating because I'm sad. I honestly just don't know why it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens when you are feeling emotional or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: Emotional or overwhelmed? Well, I suppose everyone gets overwhelmed sometimes, don't they? The world is just moving so fast. But as for the eating... I really couldn't say if that's the trigger. I just know I'm standing there, and the food is in front of me. \n\nIt's a bit like when you walk into a room and completely forget why you went in there. That happens to me all the time lately. Just yesterday, I went into the garage to get a Phillips head screwdriver, and I ended up staring at this old box of Christmas ornaments for twenty minutes. They were Mary's mother's, these delicate glass baubles from the 1950s. The paint is chipping on some of them, but I can't bear to throw them out. You just can't find craftsmanship like that anymore. Everything you buy at the store now is cheap plastic made overseas. It breaks if you look at it funny. \n\nI remember buying a toaster last year, and the darn thing just stopped popping up after three weeks. I had to stand there and watch it like a hawk, or else I'd end up with charcoal instead of toast. My old toaster, the one we got as a wedding present, that thing lasted thirty-five years. Heavy chrome, weighed a ton. When it finally gave out, it felt like losing a member of the family. \n\nBut, uh, overwhelmed? I don't know. I just know the eating happens, and I feel like I have no control over it once I start. I haven't really noticed if I'm feeling particularly emotional beforehand.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the act of eating the leftovers helps you feel less lonely or comforted in any way?\n\nPatient: Comforted? Less lonely? I... I don't know if I'd say that. I really don't think about it like that when it's happening. I'm just eating. \n\nThough, speaking of comfort, I did try to buy one of those weighted blankets they advertise on the television. The commercial made it look so cozy, like a big hug. But when it arrived, the box was so heavy I could barely lift it off the porch. The delivery driver, a nice young man named Kevin—he always waves when he drops off a package—he had to help me drag it inside. I tried sleeping under it for one night, and I felt like a panini in a sandwich press. Just pinned to the mattress. I couldn't even turn over to reach my water glass on the nightstand. \n\nI always keep a glass of water there, ever since I had that terrible dry cough back in '98. The doctor told me to stay hydrated. I used to use this beautiful crystal tumbler that Mary bought at an antique shop in Vermont, but I knocked it over one night and it shattered into a million pieces. Now I just use a plastic cup from a baseball game I went to with my nephew years ago. The Mets, I think it was. Or maybe the Yankees. I'm not much for sports, honestly. The games take far too long, and the stadiums are always so crowded and noisy. I'd much rather sit on the porch with a good book. \n\nBut, what were you asking? Comfort? Loneliness? No, I don't think the food does that for me. I haven't noticed it making me feel better or less lonely. I just end up eating it all, and then I feel terrible afterwards.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating the leftovers is a way for you to deal with difficult emotions or thoughts?\n\nPatient: Difficult emotions? Well... I don't really know. Like I've been saying, I haven't really connected the eating to any specific thoughts or feelings. It just sort of happens. \n\nThough, speaking of difficult thoughts... I was trying to do the Sunday crossword puzzle yesterday. I've been doing the Times crossword for forty years, ever since Mary and I first moved into the house. We used to sit at the kitchen table with our coffee—she took hers black, I always needed a splash of half-and-half. Anyway, the puzzle yesterday was just impossible. Seven down was a five-letter word for a type of South American bird, and I just could not think of it. I even went to the library to look it up in the encyclopedia. \n\nThe library downtown has really changed, you know. They renovated it a few years ago. Took out all those lovely old oak card catalogs and put in these sleek, modern computers. I suppose it's faster, but it just doesn't smell the same. I used to love the smell of the library. Old paper and floor wax. Now it just smells like hand sanitizer and ozone. The head librarian, Mrs. Higgins, she retired right before the renovation. She always knew exactly which mystery novel I'd want to read next. Agatha Christie, mostly. I must have read *Murder on the Orient Express* half a dozen times. They made a movie out of it recently, didn't they? With that fellow with the ridiculous mustache. I didn't see it. The movies are just too loud these days. The bass rattles right in your chest. \n\nBut I'm sorry, I'm rambling again. Dealing with difficult emotions? No, I really don't think the food is about that. I just find myself eating, and I don't really know why.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens more often on days when you're thinking about your wife?\n\nPatient: Thinking about Mary? Well... I suppose I think about her every day, don't I? So it's hard to say if the eating happens *more* on those days, because every day is one of those days. \n\nJust this morning, I was looking out the kitchen window at the rose bushes. She planted those the spring of '82. We had just put up the new fence, cedar wood, smelled wonderful when it rained. She insisted on the climbing roses, the red ones—'Don Juan', she called them. They can be terribly finicky, you know. You have to prune them just right or they get all leggy and don't bloom properly. I try to keep up with them, but my knees aren't what they used to be. I went to the hardware store last week to get some new gardening shears, because my old ones got left out in the rain and rusted shut. \n\nThe fellow at the hardware store, young kid, couldn't have been more than nineteen, he tried to sell me these battery-powered clippers. Can you imagine? Recharging your garden tools like a cellular phone. I told him I prefer the manual ones. Good, honest leverage. You feel the cut that way. He just looked at me like I had two heads. It's a shame, really. Nobody appreciates the simple mechanics of things anymore. Everything has a microchip in it. I half expect my toaster to start asking me for the Wi-Fi password one of these days. \n\nBut, uh... what was it you asked? Does it happen more when I think about her? I just don't know. Like I said, she's always on my mind, but I haven't noticed if that's what makes me start eating. I just... I just eat.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about your wife right before you start eating the leftovers?\n\nPatient: Right before I start eating? I... I really couldn't tell you. I'm not usually paying attention to what's going through my head right at that exact moment. \n\nAlthough, speaking of paying attention, I was trying to watch that new documentary on the television the other night. The one about the deep ocean? It's fascinating what they can film down there now. They had these little submersible machines with cameras, going all the way down to the Mariana Trench. It's pitch black, but they have these incredibly bright lights. And the creatures! There was this one fish, looked like it was made of glass, completely transparent. You could see its little heart beating inside. \n\nI always wanted to learn to scuba dive when I was younger. We took a trip to Florida in '78, down to the Keys. Mary loved the beach, but she was terrified of the water. Wouldn't go in past her ankles. I signed up for a beginner's diving class at the hotel, but the morning of the first lesson, I woke up with a terrible earache. Turns out I had an infection, and the doctor said the pressure underwater would be excruciating. So I spent the whole week sitting under an umbrella, reading paperbacks while Mary collected seashells. We had a jar of those shells on the mantelpiece for years, until the cat knocked it off. That cat, Barnaby, he was a menace. Orange tabby. He used to sleep on top of the refrigerator and swat at you when you walked by. \n\nAnyway... no, I don't think I'm specifically thinking about Mary right before I eat the leftovers. It's not like a trigger or anything, if that's what you mean. I just... I don't know what I'm thinking about. I just start eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you start eating the leftovers when you're having thoughts about your wife?\n\nPatient: I... I really don't think so. It's incredible. Looks like another planet entirely. All those striped rock formations. We had to wait three days in this tiny motel for the mechanic to get a new alternator shipped in. But anyway, yes, I just can't seem to make myself stop eating it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after you've finished eating the leftovers?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Well, I suppose so... yes, I do. I feel just terrible about it. Because, you know, my daughter went to all that trouble to make it. She works so hard, she's a manager down at the bank on 4th Street. The one with the big clock out front? That clock hasn't told the right time since the hurricane in '04, but they refuse to fix it. Said it's a historical landmark or something. I remember when they put it up, actually. I was just a boy, maybe ten years old. My father took me down there to watch the dedication. He was wearing his good suit, the gray one with the pinstripes. He only wore that for church and weddings. \n\nBut yes, I feel awful when I realize I've eaten three days' worth of food in one sitting. And then I have to figure out what to tell her when she asks how I liked the leftovers. I can't exactly say I ate the whole casserole in twenty minutes while standing barefoot in the kitchen.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel anxious, stressed, or upset before these episodes of eating leftovers?\n\nPatient: Anxious? Or stressed? Hmm, I don't really think so. I haven't noticed feeling particularly upset before it happens. Usually, the evenings are pretty quiet. I sit in my armchair—the brown leather one I got from Sears back when they still had that big catalog, you remember those? The pages were so thin, like tissue paper. My sister used to cut out the pictures of the dresses and pretend they were paper dolls. We didn't have much money for toys back then, so you had to make your own fun. \n\nBut no, I'm usually just sitting there watching television. Mostly those old Westerns on channel 4. Or maybe reading the evening paper, though they've raised the subscription price twice this year already. The delivery boy, Tommy, he's a good kid, rides his bike rain or shine. His mother works at the bakery, the one that makes those fantastic almond cookies. Have you ever had them? They have a little dollop of jam right in the center. But anyway, no, I wouldn't say I feel stressed or upset before I head into the kitchen. I'm usually just feeling perfectly fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel hungry during your regular meals throughout the day?\n\nPatient: Hungry during my regular meals? Well, I suppose I get a little hungry, like anyone would. But I wouldn't say I'm starving. I usually have a bowl of oatmeal in the morning. I've been eating the same brand of oatmeal for... oh, it must be forty years now. The one with the man in the hat on the box. Though they changed the box design a few years ago. Made him look younger, which I think is just silly. Why does an oatmeal mascot need to look younger? It's not like he's going out on a date. \n\nMy neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, she went on a date last week. First one since her husband passed in '98. She met the fellow at the community center during bingo night. They went to that Italian place downtown, the one with the red and white checkered tablecloths. She said he ordered the veal and didn't even offer her a bite! Can you imagine? I was raised to always share, especially if you're trying to make a good impression. My mother would have tanned my hide if I was that rude. She was a strict woman, my mother. But fair. She always made sure we had a hot meal on the table, even when things were tight. \n\nSo, I don't know, I guess I eat normally during the day. I don't really pay that much attention to how hungry I am, to be honest with you.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating the leftovers makes you feel better emotionally after you've started?\n\nPatient: Feel better emotionally? Well, I'm not really sure about that. I haven't really thought about it that way. I mean, it tastes good, of course. My daughter's casserole is wonderful. She puts those little crispy onions on top, you know the ones that come in the can? We used to have those every Thanksgiving when my wife was alive. She made the best green bean casserole. Everyone always asked for her recipe, but she wouldn't give it out. Said it was a family secret. Though, honestly, I think she just read it off the back of the soup can. \n\nBut she was a wonderful cook, my Mary. We met at a church picnic, actually. She was wearing a yellow sundress and had a ribbon in her hair. I spilled lemonade all over my shoes trying to talk to her. They were white bucks, too, practically ruined them. But she just laughed, a real genuine laugh, and handed me a napkin. We were married for forty-two years. The house feels awfully big without her, especially in the evenings. The silence can be a bit heavy sometimes. \n\nBut anyway, do I feel better? I don't know if I'd say better. I just... I don't really know, honestly. I've never really paid attention to how I feel while I'm doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of how much you eat when you're standing in the kitchen with the leftovers?\n\nPatient: In control? Well, that's the whole reason I'm here, really. Because once I start picking at those leftovers late at night... it feels like the sensible part of me is standing right there saying, \"That’s enough, Alejandro.\" But my hand just keeps going back for more anyway. It's like it just has a mind of its own. \n\nYou know, speaking of my hands, I've noticed my knuckles getting so stiff lately. My grandfather had terrible hands by the time he was my age. He used to work down at the shipyards, building those massive cargo ships. He'd come home smelling like motor oil and salt water, and he'd sit in his chair and try to carve these little wooden boats for me, but his fingers were just so bent out of shape from the labor. He used this beautiful old pocket knife with a bone handle. I still have it, actually, sitting in the top drawer of my dresser. Right next to my tie clips. \n\nDo men even wear tie clips anymore? You look at the news anchors on the television today, and half of them aren't even wearing ties at all. It just looks sloppy, if you ask me. A man should take pride in his appearance. My grandfather always wore a full suit to church, even in the dead of summer when the humidity was unbearable... \n\nBut, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, the food. Right. But yes, to answer your question, I don't feel in control. I hear myself saying to stop, but I just can't seem to stop my hand from reaching back into the container.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating the leftovers is a way for you to cope with any feelings of loneliness or emptiness you might be experiencing, especially in the evenings?\n\nPatient: Loneliness? Well, I suppose the house is quiet, like I said. Empty. But I don't know if I'm eating because of that. I haven't really sat down and analyzed it. I just know I'm standing there in the kitchen, and it happens. \n\nSpeaking of the kitchen, I really need to get that leaky faucet fixed. It just goes drip, drip, drip all night long. It's driving me absolutely up the wall. I tried to tighten it myself with a wrench, but I think I just made it worse. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he's pretty handy with tools. He built a whole new deck on the back of his house last summer. Beautiful cedar wood. Smelled wonderful for weeks. We used to sit out there and have a beer on Friday evenings. He drinks that dark stout, the kind that looks like motor oil. I never could develop a taste for it. I prefer a crisp pilsner, myself. \n\nMy father used to drink pilsner. He'd have one every Sunday afternoon while listening to the baseball game on the radio. The crack of the bat, the crowd cheering... it's a sound that just takes me right back to being ten years old. We didn't have a television until I was in high school, so the radio was our whole world. \n\nBut, uh, coping with loneliness? I really couldn't say for sure. I just know I eat the leftovers, and I can't seem to stop once I start. I don't know if there's a deep emotional reason behind it or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens when you're feeling particularly sad or missing your wife?\n\nPatient: Sad? Missing Mary? I mean, I miss her every day, of course. It's been three years, but sometimes it feels like yesterday. But as for the eating... I really haven't made a connection like that. I just find myself at the fridge. \n\nThough, speaking of the fridge, I really need to clean it out this weekend. There's a jar of pickles in the back that's been there since, well, I don't even know when. Bread and butter pickles. Mary loved those. She'd put them on sandwiches, chop them up into potato salad... her potato salad was famous at the church potlucks. She used a little bit of mustard, which I always thought was the secret ingredient. \n\nMy mother, on the other hand, she made a terrible potato salad. Just awful. Too much mayonnaise, and she'd put celery in it. I can't stand celery. It's like eating crunchy water with strings. I remember one time, I must have been about seven, I tried to hide my celery under my napkin at dinner. My father caught me, of course. He had eyes in the back of his head, I swear. He made me sit there until I ate every last piece. It took me two hours. I missed my favorite radio program, 'The Lone Ranger.' \n\nThey don't make shows like that anymore, do they? Everything on television now is just so loud and fast. It gives me a headache. I usually just watch the weather channel before bed. \n\nBut, uh, what were you asking? Oh, if I eat when I'm sad. Like I said, I really couldn't tell you. I haven't noticed if I'm feeling a certain way when it happens. I just know I end up eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat the leftovers when you're feeling sad or thinking about your wife?\n\nPatient: I thought... didn't you just ask me that, Doctor? Maybe I'm getting my wires crossed. But no, like I said just a moment ago, I really couldn't say if it's because I'm sad. I haven't noticed any sort of pattern like that. I just find myself eating. \n\nAlthough, you know, having her on my mind right now... I was actually just looking at this old photograph of her on the mantelpiece yesterday. She always had this old Kodak camera with her. The kind with those little flash cubes that would spin around and pop? She took pictures of absolutely everything. Every holiday, every bird that landed on the birdbath. We used to take the little plastic canisters of film down to the pharmacy on Main Street to get them developed. Mr. Gable ran the place back then. Tall, gangly fellow, always wore a bow tie and suspenders. He kept a big glass jar of those butterscotch candies on the counter, the ones wrapped in the yellow cellophane. I haven't seen those candies in years. I wonder if they even make them anymore? They used to get terribly stuck in my back molars. Dental work is just outrageously expensive nowadays, anyway. My dentist, Dr. Evans, he actually just retired last month and bought an RV to drive down to Florida. Can you imagine driving a vehicle that big on the interstate? \n\nBut anyway, I'm sorry. To answer your question again, no, I don't think I'm eating because I'm sad. I honestly just don't know why it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens when you are feeling emotional or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: Emotional or overwhelmed? Well, I suppose everyone gets overwhelmed sometimes, don't they? The world is just moving so fast. But as for the eating... I really couldn't say if that's the trigger. I just know I'm standing there, and the food is in front of me. \n\nIt's a bit like when you walk into a room and completely forget why you went in there. That happens to me all the time lately. Just yesterday, I went into the garage to get a Phillips head screwdriver, and I ended up staring at this old box of Christmas ornaments for twenty minutes. They were Mary's mother's, these delicate glass baubles from the 1950s. The paint is chipping on some of them, but I can't bear to throw them out. You just can't find craftsmanship like that anymore. Everything you buy at the store now is cheap plastic made overseas. It breaks if you look at it funny. \n\nI remember buying a toaster last year, and the darn thing just stopped popping up after three weeks. I had to stand there and watch it like a hawk, or else I'd end up with charcoal instead of toast. My old toaster, the one we got as a wedding present, that thing lasted thirty-five years. Heavy chrome, weighed a ton. When it finally gave out, it felt like losing a member of the family. \n\nBut, uh, overwhelmed? I don't know. I just know the eating happens, and I feel like I have no control over it once I start. I haven't really noticed if I'm feeling particularly emotional beforehand.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the act of eating the leftovers helps you feel less lonely or comforted in any way?\n\nPatient: Comforted? Less lonely? I... I don't know if I'd say that. I really don't think about it like that when it's happening. I'm just eating. \n\nThough, speaking of comfort, I did try to buy one of those weighted blankets they advertise on the television. The commercial made it look so cozy, like a big hug. But when it arrived, the box was so heavy I could barely lift it off the porch. The delivery driver, a nice young man named Kevin—he always waves when he drops off a package—he had to help me drag it inside. I tried sleeping under it for one night, and I felt like a panini in a sandwich press. Just pinned to the mattress. I couldn't even turn over to reach my water glass on the nightstand. \n\nI always keep a glass of water there, ever since I had that terrible dry cough back in '98. The doctor told me to stay hydrated. I used to use this beautiful crystal tumbler that Mary bought at an antique shop in Vermont, but I knocked it over one night and it shattered into a million pieces. Now I just use a plastic cup from a baseball game I went to with my nephew years ago. The Mets, I think it was. Or maybe the Yankees. I'm not much for sports, honestly. The games take far too long, and the stadiums are always so crowded and noisy. I'd much rather sit on the porch with a good book. \n\nBut, what were you asking? Comfort? Loneliness? No, I don't think the food does that for me. I haven't noticed it making me feel better or less lonely. I just end up eating it all, and then I feel terrible afterwards.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating the leftovers is a way for you to deal with difficult emotions or thoughts?\n\nPatient: Difficult emotions? Well... I don't really know. Like I've been saying, I haven't really connected the eating to any specific thoughts or feelings. It just sort of happens. \n\nThough, speaking of difficult thoughts... I was trying to do the Sunday crossword puzzle yesterday. I've been doing the Times crossword for forty years, ever since Mary and I first moved into the house. We used to sit at the kitchen table with our coffee—she took hers black, I always needed a splash of half-and-half. Anyway, the puzzle yesterday was just impossible. Seven down was a five-letter word for a type of South American bird, and I just could not think of it. I even went to the library to look it up in the encyclopedia. \n\nThe library downtown has really changed, you know. They renovated it a few years ago. Took out all those lovely old oak card catalogs and put in these sleek, modern computers. I suppose it's faster, but it just doesn't smell the same. I used to love the smell of the library. Old paper and floor wax. Now it just smells like hand sanitizer and ozone. The head librarian, Mrs. Higgins, she retired right before the renovation. She always knew exactly which mystery novel I'd want to read next. Agatha Christie, mostly. I must have read *Murder on the Orient Express* half a dozen times. They made a movie out of it recently, didn't they? With that fellow with the ridiculous mustache. I didn't see it. The movies are just too loud these days. The bass rattles right in your chest. \n\nBut I'm sorry, I'm rambling again. Dealing with difficult emotions? No, I really don't think the food is about that. I just find myself eating, and I don't really know why.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens more often on days when you're thinking about your wife?\n\nPatient: Thinking about Mary? Well... I suppose I think about her every day, don't I? So it's hard to say if the eating happens *more* on those days, because every day is one of those days. \n\nJust this morning, I was looking out the kitchen window at the rose bushes. She planted those the spring of '82. We had just put up the new fence, cedar wood, smelled wonderful when it rained. She insisted on the climbing roses, the red ones—'Don Juan', she called them. They can be terribly finicky, you know. You have to prune them just right or they get all leggy and don't bloom properly. I try to keep up with them, but my knees aren't what they used to be. I went to the hardware store last week to get some new gardening shears, because my old ones got left out in the rain and rusted shut. \n\nThe fellow at the hardware store, young kid, couldn't have been more than nineteen, he tried to sell me these battery-powered clippers. Can you imagine? Recharging your garden tools like a cellular phone. I told him I prefer the manual ones. Good, honest leverage. You feel the cut that way. He just looked at me like I had two heads. It's a shame, really. Nobody appreciates the simple mechanics of things anymore. Everything has a microchip in it. I half expect my toaster to start asking me for the Wi-Fi password one of these days. \n\nBut, uh... what was it you asked? Does it happen more when I think about her? I just don't know. Like I said, she's always on my mind, but I haven't noticed if that's what makes me start eating. I just... I just eat.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about your wife right before you start eating the leftovers?\n\nPatient: Right before I start eating? I... I really couldn't tell you. I'm not usually paying attention to what's going through my head right at that exact moment. \n\nAlthough, speaking of paying attention, I was trying to watch that new documentary on the television the other night. The one about the deep ocean? It's fascinating what they can film down there now. They had these little submersible machines with cameras, going all the way down to the Mariana Trench. It's pitch black, but they have these incredibly bright lights. And the creatures! There was this one fish, looked like it was made of glass, completely transparent. You could see its little heart beating inside. \n\nI always wanted to learn to scuba dive when I was younger. We took a trip to Florida in '78, down to the Keys. Mary loved the beach, but she was terrified of the water. Wouldn't go in past her ankles. I signed up for a beginner's diving class at the hotel, but the morning of the first lesson, I woke up with a terrible earache. Turns out I had an infection, and the doctor said the pressure underwater would be excruciating. So I spent the whole week sitting under an umbrella, reading paperbacks while Mary collected seashells. We had a jar of those shells on the mantelpiece for years, until the cat knocked it off. That cat, Barnaby, he was a menace. Orange tabby. He used to sleep on top of the refrigerator and swat at you when you walked by. \n\nAnyway... no, I don't think I'm specifically thinking about Mary right before I eat the leftovers. It's not like a trigger or anything, if that's what you mean. I just... I don't know what I'm thinking about. I just start eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you start eating the leftovers when you're having thoughts about your wife?\n\nPatient: I... I really don't think so. I mean, we've been going over this, and I just don't see a connection between thinking about Mary and the eating. It's just not like that. \n\nThough, you know, talking about Mary... I was going through some old boxes in the attic this weekend. I was actually looking for my winter coat. The one with the wool lining. The weatherman said we're supposed to get an early frost this year. The Farmer's Almanac predicted it too, actually. I always buy a copy at the grocery store checkout in November. Anyway, I was looking for the coat, and I found this old photo album from our trip to Yellowstone. 1994, I think it was. We drove the whole way in that old station wagon. The one with the faux wood paneling on the sides. It got terrible gas mileage, just awful. We broke down somewhere in South Dakota, right near the Badlands. \n\nHave you ever been to the Badlands? It's incredible. Looks like another planet entirely. All those striped rock formations. We had to wait three days in this tiny motel for the mechanic to get a new alternator shipped in. The motel only had three channels on the television, and one of them was just weather reports. We played a lot of gin rummy that week. Mary always won. She had a mind like a steel trap for cards. \n\nBut, no, to answer your question... I don't think thoughts of her are what make me start eating the leftovers. I just find myself doing it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Doctor, I feel like we're going around in circles here. You keep asking me if thinking about Mary is what makes me eat the leftovers, and I keep telling you, I really don't think it is. I'm just not making that connection. 

It's funny, though, talking about circles... I was watching the neighbor's dog this morning. A golden retriever, beautiful coat on him. He was chasing his tail in the front yard for a solid ten minutes. Just spinning and spinning. It reminded me of this carnival ride Mary and I went on at the county fair back in, oh, must have been '75. The Tilt-A-Whirl, they called it. You sit in these little carts and they just spin you around until you're dizzy. I never had much of a stomach for those things. I prefer the Ferris wheel. Nice, slow, you get a good view of the whole fairground. You could see the livestock barns from up there, and the tents where they judged the pies. Mary won a blue ribbon for her cherry pie one year. She used tart cherries, not the sweet ones, and a lattice crust. Said the secret was keeping the butter ice cold before you mixed it in. 

But anyway, to get back to what you were asking... no. I don't think I start eating because I'm thinking about her. I just... I don't know why I do it. I just find myself eating.

Made him look younger, which I think is just silly. Why does an oatmeal mascot need to look younger? She said he ordered the veal and didn't even offer her a bite! Can you imagine? I was raised to always share, especially if you're trying to make a good impression. My mother would have tanned my hide if I was that rude. She was a strict woman, my mother. But fair. She always made sure we had a hot meal on the table, even when things were tight. \n\nSo, I don't know, I guess I eat normally during the day. I don't really pay that much attention to how hungry I am, to be honest with you.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating the leftovers makes you feel better emotionally after you've started?\n\nPatient: Feel better emotionally? Well, I'm not really sure about that. I haven't really thought about it that way. I mean, it tastes good, of course. My daughter's casserole is wonderful. She puts those little crispy onions on top, you know the ones that come in the can? We used to have those every Thanksgiving when my wife was alive. She made the best green bean casserole. Everyone always asked for her recipe, but she wouldn't give it out. Said it was a family secret. She was wearing a yellow sundress and had a ribbon in her hair. I spilled lemonade all over my shoes trying to talk to her. They were white bucks, too, practically ruined them. I've never really paid attention to how I feel while I'm doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of how much you eat when you're standing in the kitchen with the leftovers?\n\nPatient: In control? He used to work down at the shipyards, building those massive cargo ships. He'd come home smelling like motor oil and salt water, and he'd sit in his chair and try to carve these little wooden boats for me, but his fingers were just so bent out of shape from the labor. He used this beautiful old pocket knife with a bone handle. I still have it, actually, sitting in the top drawer of my dresser. Right next to my tie clips. \n\nDo men even wear tie clips anymore? He built a whole new deck on the back of his house last summer. Beautiful cedar wood. Smelled wonderful for weeks. We used to sit out there and have a beer on Friday evenings. I just find myself at the fridge. \n\nThough, speaking of the fridge, I really need to clean it out this weekend. There's a jar of pickles in the back that's been there since, well, I don't even know when. Bread and butter pickles. Mary loved those. She'd put them on sandwiches, chop them up into potato salad... her potato salad was famous at the church potlucks. I just find myself eating. \n\nAlthough, you know, having her on my mind right now... I was actually just looking at this old photograph of her on the mantelpiece yesterday. She always had this old Kodak camera with her. The kind with those little flash cubes that would spin around and pop? She took pictures of absolutely everything. Every holiday, every bird that landed on the birdbath. We used to take the little plastic canisters of film down to the pharmacy on Main Street to get them developed. Mr. Gable ran the place back then. Tall, gangly fellow, always wore a bow tie and suspenders. He kept a big glass jar of those butterscotch candies on the counter, the ones wrapped in the yellow cellophane. I haven't seen those candies in years. I wonder if they even make them anymore? They used to get terribly stuck in my back molars. Dental work is just outrageously expensive nowadays, anyway. To answer your question again, no, I don't think I'm eating because I'm sad. Well... I don't really know. Like I've been saying, I haven't really connected the eating to any specific thoughts or feelings. It just sort of happens. \n\nThough, speaking of difficult thoughts... I was trying to do the Sunday crossword puzzle yesterday. I've been doing the Times crossword for forty years, ever since Mary and I first moved into the house. We used to sit at the kitchen table with our coffee—she took hers black, I always needed a splash of half-and-half. Anyway, the puzzle yesterday was just impossible. Seven down was a five-letter word for a type of South American bird, and I just could not think of it. I even went to the library to look it up in the encyclopedia. \n\nThe library downtown has really changed, you know. They renovated it a few years ago. Turns out I had an infection, and the doctor said the pressure underwater would be excruciating. So I spent the whole week sitting under an umbrella, reading paperbacks while Mary collected seashells. We had a jar of those shells on the mantelpiece for years, until the cat knocked it off. I was going through some old boxes in the attic this weekend. I was actually looking for my winter coat. The one with the wool lining. The weatherman said we're supposed to get an early frost this year. The Farmer's Almanac predicted it too, actually. I always buy a copy at the grocery store checkout in November. Anyway, I was looking for the coat, and I found this old photo album from our trip to Yellowstone. 1994, I think it was. We drove the whole way in that old station wagon. She had a mind like a steel trap for cards. \n\nBut, no, to answer your question... I don't think thoughts of her are what make me start eating the leftovers. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, actually. That's exactly it. What brings me in is that once I start picking at the leftovers late at night, it feels like the sensible part of me is saying, "That's enough, Alejandro," but my hand just keeps going back for more anyway. 

It's like I'm on autopilot or something. Speaking of autopilot, did you see that article in the paper about those new cars they're testing? The ones that drive themselves? It just boggles the mind. I remember when I bought my first car, a '68 Chevy Impala. Beautiful machine. Powder blue. I had to learn how to drive a stick shift on the hills in San Francisco. Talk about nerve-wracking! My older brother taught me. He was a mechanic in the Navy, stationed out in San Diego for a while. He used to tell these stories about the aircraft carriers... massive things, like floating cities. He said they had a dining hall that served thousands of meals a day. Imagine the leftovers they must have had! They probably just tossed it all overboard for the seagulls. Seagulls will eat anything, you know. I was at the pier once, eating a hot dog, and one swooped right down and snatched it out of the bun. Just incredible. 

But yes, to answer your question... once I start eating those leftovers, I just can't seem to make myself stop, even when I know I should.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, hello doctor. I suppose what brings me in is... well, it happens late at night. Once I start picking at the leftovers, I just keep going. It is usually the casseroles my daughter drops off. She makes this green bean thing with those crispy fried onions on top. You know the ones that come in the little plastic tubs? They had a buy-one-get-one free deal at the market down the street, the one that used to be a hardware store back in the eighties. I used to buy all my tools there before they paved over the lot and turned it into a strip mall. \n\nAnyway, the issue is that when I am standing there with the leftovers in the evening, it feels like the sensible part of my brain is saying, That is enough, Alejandro. But my hand just keeps going back for more anyway. I just keep reaching into the container. And standing there in the kitchen doing that, I noticed the linoleum is starting to peel up near the sink, which is a whole other headache because I am retired now and getting down on my knees to fix the floor just is not as easy as it used to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can't stop yourself from eating the leftovers once you start?\n\nPatient: Yes, exactly. Like I was just saying, I really can't seem to stop myself. The sensible part of me is right there saying, 'That is enough, Alejandro,' but my hand just keeps going back for more anyway. It's like my hand has a mind of its own once I start. \n\nWhich is frustrating because then I have to wash the container all over again, and I really need to switch to those glass storage bowls. The plastic ones my daughter uses get so stained, especially if there's any tomato sauce involved. She brought over a baked ziti a few weeks ago and the plastic tub is still bright orange. I tried scrubbing it with baking soda, which is usually a miracle worker. My grandmother swore by baking soda. She used to use it for everything, even brushing her teeth. She lived to be ninety-two and never had a single cavity, if you can believe that. But anyway, yes, I just can't seem to make myself stop eating it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after you've finished eating the leftovers?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Well, I suppose so... yes, I do. I feel just terrible about it. Because, you know, my daughter went to all that trouble to make it. She works so hard, she's a manager down at the bank on 4th Street. The one with the big clock out front? That clock hasn't told the right time since the hurricane in '04, but they refuse to fix it. Said it's a historical landmark or something. I remember when they put it up, actually. I was just a boy, maybe ten years old. My father took me down there to watch the dedication. He was wearing his good suit, the gray one with the pinstripes. He only wore that for church and weddings. \n\nBut yes, I feel awful when I realize I've eaten three days' worth of food in one sitting. And then I have to figure out what to tell her when she asks how I liked the leftovers. I can't exactly say I ate the whole casserole in twenty minutes while standing barefoot in the kitchen.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel anxious, stressed, or upset before these episodes of eating leftovers?\n\nPatient: Anxious? Or stressed? Hmm, I don't really think so. I haven't noticed feeling particularly upset before it happens. Usually, the evenings are pretty quiet. I sit in my armchair—the brown leather one I got from Sears back when they still had that big catalog, you remember those? The pages were so thin, like tissue paper. My sister used to cut out the pictures of the dresses and pretend they were paper dolls. We didn't have much money for toys back then, so you had to make your own fun. \n\nBut no, I'm usually just sitting there watching television. Mostly those old Westerns on channel 4. Or maybe reading the evening paper, though they've raised the subscription price twice this year already. The delivery boy, Tommy, he's a good kid, rides his bike rain or shine. His mother works at the bakery, the one that makes those fantastic almond cookies. Have you ever had them? They have a little dollop of jam right in the center. But anyway, no, I wouldn't say I feel stressed or upset before I head into the kitchen. I'm usually just feeling perfectly fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel hungry during your regular meals throughout the day?\n\nPatient: Hungry during my regular meals? Well, I suppose I get a little hungry, like anyone would. But I wouldn't say I'm starving. I usually have a bowl of oatmeal in the morning. I've been eating the same brand of oatmeal for... oh, it must be forty years now. The one with the man in the hat on the box. Though they changed the box design a few years ago. Made him look younger, which I think is just silly. Why does an oatmeal mascot need to look younger? It's not like he's going out on a date. \n\nMy neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, she went on a date last week. First one since her husband passed in '98. She met the fellow at the community center during bingo night. They went to that Italian place downtown, the one with the red and white checkered tablecloths. She said he ordered the veal and didn't even offer her a bite! Can you imagine? I was raised to always share, especially if you're trying to make a good impression. My mother would have tanned my hide if I was that rude. She was a strict woman, my mother. But fair. She always made sure we had a hot meal on the table, even when things were tight. \n\nSo, I don't know, I guess I eat normally during the day. I don't really pay that much attention to how hungry I am, to be honest with you.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating the leftovers makes you feel better emotionally after you've started?\n\nPatient: Feel better emotionally? Well, I'm not really sure about that. I haven't really thought about it that way. I mean, it tastes good, of course. My daughter's casserole is wonderful. She puts those little crispy onions on top, you know the ones that come in the can? We used to have those every Thanksgiving when my wife was alive. She made the best green bean casserole. Everyone always asked for her recipe, but she wouldn't give it out. Said it was a family secret. Though, honestly, I think she just read it off the back of the soup can. \n\nBut she was a wonderful cook, my Mary. We met at a church picnic, actually. She was wearing a yellow sundress and had a ribbon in her hair. I spilled lemonade all over my shoes trying to talk to her. They were white bucks, too, practically ruined them. But she just laughed, a real genuine laugh, and handed me a napkin. We were married for forty-two years. The house feels awfully big without her, especially in the evenings. The silence can be a bit heavy sometimes. \n\nBut anyway, do I feel better? I don't know if I'd say better. I just... I don't really know, honestly. I've never really paid attention to how I feel while I'm doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of how much you eat when you're standing in the kitchen with the leftovers?\n\nPatient: In control? Well, that's the whole reason I'm here, really. Because once I start picking at those leftovers late at night... it feels like the sensible part of me is standing right there saying, \"That’s enough, Alejandro.\" But my hand just keeps going back for more anyway. It's like it just has a mind of its own. \n\nYou know, speaking of my hands, I've noticed my knuckles getting so stiff lately. My grandfather had terrible hands by the time he was my age. He used to work down at the shipyards, building those massive cargo ships. He'd come home smelling like motor oil and salt water, and he'd sit in his chair and try to carve these little wooden boats for me, but his fingers were just so bent out of shape from the labor. He used this beautiful old pocket knife with a bone handle. I still have it, actually, sitting in the top drawer of my dresser. Right next to my tie clips. \n\nDo men even wear tie clips anymore? You look at the news anchors on the television today, and half of them aren't even wearing ties at all. It just looks sloppy, if you ask me. A man should take pride in his appearance. My grandfather always wore a full suit to church, even in the dead of summer when the humidity was unbearable... \n\nBut, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, the food. Right. But yes, to answer your question, I don't feel in control. I hear myself saying to stop, but I just can't seem to stop my hand from reaching back into the container.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating the leftovers is a way for you to cope with any feelings of loneliness or emptiness you might be experiencing, especially in the evenings?\n\nPatient: Loneliness? Well, I suppose the house is quiet, like I said. Empty. But I don't know if I'm eating because of that. I haven't really sat down and analyzed it. I just know I'm standing there in the kitchen, and it happens. \n\nSpeaking of the kitchen, I really need to get that leaky faucet fixed. It just goes drip, drip, drip all night long. It's driving me absolutely up the wall. I tried to tighten it myself with a wrench, but I think I just made it worse. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he's pretty handy with tools. He built a whole new deck on the back of his house last summer. Beautiful cedar wood. Smelled wonderful for weeks. We used to sit out there and have a beer on Friday evenings. He drinks that dark stout, the kind that looks like motor oil. I never could develop a taste for it. I prefer a crisp pilsner, myself. \n\nMy father used to drink pilsner. He'd have one every Sunday afternoon while listening to the baseball game on the radio. The crack of the bat, the crowd cheering... it's a sound that just takes me right back to being ten years old. We didn't have a television until I was in high school, so the radio was our whole world. \n\nBut, uh, coping with loneliness? I really couldn't say for sure. I just know I eat the leftovers, and I can't seem to stop once I start. I don't know if there's a deep emotional reason behind it or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens when you're feeling particularly sad or missing your wife?\n\nPatient: Sad? Missing Mary? I mean, I miss her every day, of course. It's been three years, but sometimes it feels like yesterday. But as for the eating... I really haven't made a connection like that. I just find myself at the fridge. \n\nThough, speaking of the fridge, I really need to clean it out this weekend. There's a jar of pickles in the back that's been there since, well, I don't even know when. Bread and butter pickles. Mary loved those. She'd put them on sandwiches, chop them up into potato salad... her potato salad was famous at the church potlucks. She used a little bit of mustard, which I always thought was the secret ingredient. \n\nMy mother, on the other hand, she made a terrible potato salad. Just awful. Too much mayonnaise, and she'd put celery in it. I can't stand celery. It's like eating crunchy water with strings. I remember one time, I must have been about seven, I tried to hide my celery under my napkin at dinner. My father caught me, of course. He had eyes in the back of his head, I swear. He made me sit there until I ate every last piece. It took me two hours. I missed my favorite radio program, 'The Lone Ranger.' \n\nThey don't make shows like that anymore, do they? Everything on television now is just so loud and fast. It gives me a headache. I usually just watch the weather channel before bed. \n\nBut, uh, what were you asking? Oh, if I eat when I'm sad. Like I said, I really couldn't tell you. I haven't noticed if I'm feeling a certain way when it happens. I just know I end up eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat the leftovers when you're feeling sad or thinking about your wife?\n\nPatient: I thought... didn't you just ask me that, Doctor? Maybe I'm getting my wires crossed. But no, like I said just a moment ago, I really couldn't say if it's because I'm sad. I haven't noticed any sort of pattern like that. I just find myself eating. \n\nAlthough, you know, having her on my mind right now... I was actually just looking at this old photograph of her on the mantelpiece yesterday. She always had this old Kodak camera with her. The kind with those little flash cubes that would spin around and pop? She took pictures of absolutely everything. Every holiday, every bird that landed on the birdbath. We used to take the little plastic canisters of film down to the pharmacy on Main Street to get them developed. Mr. Gable ran the place back then. Tall, gangly fellow, always wore a bow tie and suspenders. He kept a big glass jar of those butterscotch candies on the counter, the ones wrapped in the yellow cellophane. I haven't seen those candies in years. I wonder if they even make them anymore? They used to get terribly stuck in my back molars. Dental work is just outrageously expensive nowadays, anyway. My dentist, Dr. Evans, he actually just retired last month and bought an RV to drive down to Florida. Can you imagine driving a vehicle that big on the interstate? \n\nBut anyway, I'm sorry. To answer your question again, no, I don't think I'm eating because I'm sad. I honestly just don't know why it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens when you are feeling emotional or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: Emotional or overwhelmed? Well, I suppose everyone gets overwhelmed sometimes, don't they? The world is just moving so fast. But as for the eating... I really couldn't say if that's the trigger. I just know I'm standing there, and the food is in front of me. \n\nIt's a bit like when you walk into a room and completely forget why you went in there. That happens to me all the time lately. Just yesterday, I went into the garage to get a Phillips head screwdriver, and I ended up staring at this old box of Christmas ornaments for twenty minutes. They were Mary's mother's, these delicate glass baubles from the 1950s. The paint is chipping on some of them, but I can't bear to throw them out. You just can't find craftsmanship like that anymore. Everything you buy at the store now is cheap plastic made overseas. It breaks if you look at it funny. \n\nI remember buying a toaster last year, and the darn thing just stopped popping up after three weeks. I had to stand there and watch it like a hawk, or else I'd end up with charcoal instead of toast. My old toaster, the one we got as a wedding present, that thing lasted thirty-five years. Heavy chrome, weighed a ton. When it finally gave out, it felt like losing a member of the family. \n\nBut, uh, overwhelmed? I don't know. I just know the eating happens, and I feel like I have no control over it once I start. I haven't really noticed if I'm feeling particularly emotional beforehand.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the act of eating the leftovers helps you feel less lonely or comforted in any way?\n\nPatient: Comforted? Less lonely? I... I don't know if I'd say that. I really don't think about it like that when it's happening. I'm just eating. \n\nThough, speaking of comfort, I did try to buy one of those weighted blankets they advertise on the television. The commercial made it look so cozy, like a big hug. But when it arrived, the box was so heavy I could barely lift it off the porch. The delivery driver, a nice young man named Kevin—he always waves when he drops off a package—he had to help me drag it inside. I tried sleeping under it for one night, and I felt like a panini in a sandwich press. Just pinned to the mattress. I couldn't even turn over to reach my water glass on the nightstand. \n\nI always keep a glass of water there, ever since I had that terrible dry cough back in '98. The doctor told me to stay hydrated. I used to use this beautiful crystal tumbler that Mary bought at an antique shop in Vermont, but I knocked it over one night and it shattered into a million pieces. Now I just use a plastic cup from a baseball game I went to with my nephew years ago. The Mets, I think it was. Or maybe the Yankees. I'm not much for sports, honestly. The games take far too long, and the stadiums are always so crowded and noisy. I'd much rather sit on the porch with a good book. \n\nBut, what were you asking? Comfort? Loneliness? No, I don't think the food does that for me. I haven't noticed it making me feel better or less lonely. I just end up eating it all, and then I feel terrible afterwards.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating the leftovers is a way for you to deal with difficult emotions or thoughts?\n\nPatient: Difficult emotions? Well... I don't really know. Like I've been saying, I haven't really connected the eating to any specific thoughts or feelings. It just sort of happens. \n\nThough, speaking of difficult thoughts... I was trying to do the Sunday crossword puzzle yesterday. I've been doing the Times crossword for forty years, ever since Mary and I first moved into the house. We used to sit at the kitchen table with our coffee—she took hers black, I always needed a splash of half-and-half. Anyway, the puzzle yesterday was just impossible. Seven down was a five-letter word for a type of South American bird, and I just could not think of it. I even went to the library to look it up in the encyclopedia. \n\nThe library downtown has really changed, you know. They renovated it a few years ago. Took out all those lovely old oak card catalogs and put in these sleek, modern computers. I suppose it's faster, but it just doesn't smell the same. I used to love the smell of the library. Old paper and floor wax. Now it just smells like hand sanitizer and ozone. The head librarian, Mrs. Higgins, she retired right before the renovation. She always knew exactly which mystery novel I'd want to read next. Agatha Christie, mostly. I must have read *Murder on the Orient Express* half a dozen times. They made a movie out of it recently, didn't they? With that fellow with the ridiculous mustache. I didn't see it. The movies are just too loud these days. The bass rattles right in your chest. \n\nBut I'm sorry, I'm rambling again. Dealing with difficult emotions? No, I really don't think the food is about that. I just find myself eating, and I don't really know why.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens more often on days when you're thinking about your wife?\n\nPatient: Thinking about Mary? Well... I suppose I think about her every day, don't I? So it's hard to say if the eating happens *more* on those days, because every day is one of those days. \n\nJust this morning, I was looking out the kitchen window at the rose bushes. She planted those the spring of '82. We had just put up the new fence, cedar wood, smelled wonderful when it rained. She insisted on the climbing roses, the red ones—'Don Juan', she called them. They can be terribly finicky, you know. You have to prune them just right or they get all leggy and don't bloom properly. I try to keep up with them, but my knees aren't what they used to be. I went to the hardware store last week to get some new gardening shears, because my old ones got left out in the rain and rusted shut. \n\nThe fellow at the hardware store, young kid, couldn't have been more than nineteen, he tried to sell me these battery-powered clippers. Can you imagine? Recharging your garden tools like a cellular phone. I told him I prefer the manual ones. Good, honest leverage. You feel the cut that way. He just looked at me like I had two heads. It's a shame, really. Nobody appreciates the simple mechanics of things anymore. Everything has a microchip in it. I half expect my toaster to start asking me for the Wi-Fi password one of these days. \n\nBut, uh... what was it you asked? Does it happen more when I think about her? I just don't know. Like I said, she's always on my mind, but I haven't noticed if that's what makes me start eating. I just... I just eat.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about your wife right before you start eating the leftovers?\n\nPatient: Right before I start eating? I... I really couldn't tell you. I'm not usually paying attention to what's going through my head right at that exact moment. \n\nAlthough, speaking of paying attention, I was trying to watch that new documentary on the television the other night. The one about the deep ocean? It's fascinating what they can film down there now. They had these little submersible machines with cameras, going all the way down to the Mariana Trench. It's pitch black, but they have these incredibly bright lights. And the creatures! There was this one fish, looked like it was made of glass, completely transparent. You could see its little heart beating inside. \n\nI always wanted to learn to scuba dive when I was younger. We took a trip to Florida in '78, down to the Keys. Mary loved the beach, but she was terrified of the water. Wouldn't go in past her ankles. I signed up for a beginner's diving class at the hotel, but the morning of the first lesson, I woke up with a terrible earache. Turns out I had an infection, and the doctor said the pressure underwater would be excruciating. So I spent the whole week sitting under an umbrella, reading paperbacks while Mary collected seashells. We had a jar of those shells on the mantelpiece for years, until the cat knocked it off. That cat, Barnaby, he was a menace. Orange tabby. He used to sleep on top of the refrigerator and swat at you when you walked by. \n\nAnyway... no, I don't think I'm specifically thinking about Mary right before I eat the leftovers. It's not like a trigger or anything, if that's what you mean. I just... I don't know what I'm thinking about. I just start eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you start eating the leftovers when you're having thoughts about your wife?\n\nPatient: I... I really don't think so. I mean, we've been going over this, and I just don't see a connection between thinking about Mary and the eating. It's just not like that. \n\nThough, you know, talking about Mary... I was going through some old boxes in the attic this weekend. I was actually looking for my winter coat. The one with the wool lining. The weatherman said we're supposed to get an early frost this year. The Farmer's Almanac predicted it too, actually. I always buy a copy at the grocery store checkout in November. Anyway, I was looking for the coat, and I found this old photo album from our trip to Yellowstone. 1994, I think it was. We drove the whole way in that old station wagon. The one with the faux wood paneling on the sides. It got terrible gas mileage, just awful. We broke down somewhere in South Dakota, right near the Badlands. \n\nHave you ever been to the Badlands? It's incredible. Looks like another planet entirely. All those striped rock formations. We had to wait three days in this tiny motel for the mechanic to get a new alternator shipped in. The motel only had three channels on the television, and one of them was just weather reports. We played a lot of gin rummy that week. Mary always won. She had a mind like a steel trap for cards. \n\nBut, no, to answer your question... I don't think thoughts of her are what make me start eating the leftovers. I just find myself doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens when you are having thoughts about your wife?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I feel like we're going around in circles here. You keep asking me if thinking about Mary is what makes me eat the leftovers, and I keep telling you, I really don't think it is. I'm just not making that connection. \n\nIt's funny, though, talking about circles... I was watching the neighbor's dog this morning. A golden retriever, beautiful coat on him. He was chasing his tail in the front yard for a solid ten minutes. Just spinning and spinning. It reminded me of this carnival ride Mary and I went on at the county fair back in, oh, must have been '75. The Tilt-A-Whirl, they called it. You sit in these little carts and they just spin you around until you're dizzy. I never had much of a stomach for those things. I prefer the Ferris wheel. Nice, slow, you get a good view of the whole fairground. You could see the livestock barns from up there, and the tents where they judged the pies. Mary won a blue ribbon for her cherry pie one year. She used tart cherries, not the sweet ones, and a lattice crust. Said the secret was keeping the butter ice cold before you mixed it in. \n\nBut anyway, to get back to what you were asking... no. I don't think I start eating because I'm thinking about her. I just... I don't know why I do it. I just find myself eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to eat the leftovers once you start, even if you're not hungry anymore?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. That's exactly it. What brings me in is that once I start picking at the leftovers late at night, it feels like the sensible part of me is saying, \"That's enough, Alejandro,\" but my hand just keeps going back for more anyway. \n\nIt's like I'm on autopilot or something. Speaking of autopilot, did you see that article in the paper about those new cars they're testing? The ones that drive themselves? It just boggles the mind. I remember when I bought my first car, a '68 Chevy Impala. Beautiful machine. Powder blue. I had to learn how to drive a stick shift on the hills in San Francisco. Talk about nerve-wracking! My older brother taught me. He was a mechanic in the Navy, stationed out in San Diego for a while. He used to tell these stories about the aircraft carriers... massive things, like floating cities. He said they had a dining hall that served thousands of meals a day. Imagine the leftovers they must have had! They probably just tossed it all overboard for the seagulls. Seagulls will eat anything, you know. I was at the pier once, eating a hot dog, and one swooped right down and snatched it out of the bun. Just incredible. \n\nBut yes, to answer your question... once I start eating those leftovers, I just can't seem to make myself stop, even when I know I should.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. She always knew exactly which mystery novel I'd want to read next. Agatha Christie, mostly. I must have read *Murder on the Orient Express* half a dozen times. They made a movie out of it recently, didn't they? With that fellow with the ridiculous mustache. I didn't see it. The movies are just too loud these days. The bass rattles right in your chest. \n\nBut I'm sorry, I'm rambling again. Dealing with difficult emotions? No, I really don't think the food is about that. I just find myself eating, and I don't really know why.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens more often on days when you're thinking about your wife?\n\nPatient: Thinking about Mary? Well... I suppose I think about her every day, don't I? So it's hard to say if the eating happens *more* on those days, because every day is one of those days. \n\nJust this morning, I was looking out the kitchen window at the rose bushes. She planted those the spring of '82. We had just put up the new fence, cedar wood, smelled wonderful when it rained. She insisted on the climbing roses, the red ones—'Don Juan', she called them. They can be terribly finicky, you know. You have to prune them just right or they get all leggy and don't bloom properly. I try to keep up with them, but my knees aren't what they used to be. I went to the hardware store last week to get some new gardening shears, because my old ones got left out in the rain and rusted shut. \n\nThe fellow at the hardware store, young kid, couldn't have been more than nineteen, he tried to sell me these battery-powered clippers. Can you imagine? Recharging your garden tools like a cellular phone. I told him I prefer the manual ones. Good, honest leverage. You feel the cut that way. He just looked at me like I had two heads. It's a shame, really. Nobody appreciates the simple mechanics of things anymore. Everything has a microchip in it. I half expect my toaster to start asking me for the Wi-Fi password one of these days. \n\nBut, uh... what was it you asked? Does it happen more when I think about her? I just don't know. Like I said, she's always on my mind, but I haven't noticed if that's what makes me start eating. I just... I just eat.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about your wife right before you start eating the leftovers?\n\nPatient: Right before I start eating? I... I really couldn't tell you. I'm not usually paying attention to what's going through my head right at that exact moment. \n\nAlthough, speaking of paying attention, I was trying to watch that new documentary on the television the other night. The one about the deep ocean? It's fascinating what they can film down there now. They had these little submersible machines with cameras, going all the way down to the Mariana Trench. It's pitch black, but they have these incredibly bright lights. And the creatures! There was this one fish, looked like it was made of glass, completely transparent. You could see its little heart beating inside. \n\nI always wanted to learn to scuba dive when I was younger. We took a trip to Florida in '78, down to the Keys. Mary loved the beach, but she was terrified of the water. Wouldn't go in past her ankles. I signed up for a beginner's diving class at the hotel, but the morning of the first lesson, I woke up with a terrible earache. Turns out I had an infection, and the doctor said the pressure underwater would be excruciating. So I spent the whole week sitting under an umbrella, reading paperbacks while Mary collected seashells. We had a jar of those shells on the mantelpiece for years, until the cat knocked it off. That cat, Barnaby, he was a menace. Orange tabby. He used to sleep on top of the refrigerator and swat at you when you walked by. \n\nAnyway... no, I don't think I'm specifically thinking about Mary right before I eat the leftovers. It's not like a trigger or anything, if that's what you mean. I just... I don't know what I'm thinking about. I just start eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you start eating the leftovers when you're having thoughts about your wife?\n\nPatient: I... I really don't think so. I mean, we've been going over this, and I just don't see a connection between thinking about Mary and the eating. It's just not like that. \n\nThough, you know, talking about Mary... I was going through some old boxes in the attic this weekend. I was actually looking for my winter coat. The one with the wool lining. The weatherman said we're supposed to get an early frost this year. The Farmer's Almanac predicted it too, actually. I always buy a copy at the grocery store checkout in November. Anyway, I was looking for the coat, and I found this old photo album from our trip to Yellowstone. 1994, I think it was. We drove the whole way in that old station wagon. The one with the faux wood paneling on the sides. It got terrible gas mileage, just awful. We broke down somewhere in South Dakota, right near the Badlands. \n\nHave you ever been to the Badlands? It's incredible. Looks like another planet entirely. All those striped rock formations. We had to wait three days in this tiny motel for the mechanic to get a new alternator shipped in. The motel only had three channels on the television, and one of them was just weather reports. We played a lot of gin rummy that week. Mary always won. She had a mind like a steel trap for cards. \n\nBut, no, to answer your question... I don't think thoughts of her are what make me start eating the leftovers. I just find myself doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens when you are having thoughts about your wife?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I feel like we're going around in circles here. You keep asking me if thinking about Mary is what makes me eat the leftovers, and I keep telling you, I really don't think it is. I'm just not making that connection. \n\nIt's funny, though, talking about circles... I was watching the neighbor's dog this morning. A golden retriever, beautiful coat on him. He was chasing his tail in the front yard for a solid ten minutes. Just spinning and spinning. It reminded me of this carnival ride Mary and I went on at the county fair back in, oh, must have been '75. The Tilt-A-Whirl, they called it. You sit in these little carts and they just spin you around until you're dizzy. I never had much of a stomach for those things. I prefer the Ferris wheel. Nice, slow, you get a good view of the whole fairground. You could see the livestock barns from up there, and the tents where they judged the pies. Mary won a blue ribbon for her cherry pie one year. She used tart cherries, not the sweet ones, and a lattice crust. Said the secret was keeping the butter ice cold before you mixed it in. \n\nBut anyway, to get back to what you were asking... no. I don't think I start eating because I'm thinking about her. I just... I don't know why I do it. I just find myself eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to eat the leftovers once you start, even if you're not hungry anymore?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. That's exactly it. What brings me in is that once I start picking at the leftovers late at night, it feels like the sensible part of me is saying, \"That's enough, Alejandro,\" but my hand just keeps going back for more anyway. \n\nIt's like I'm on autopilot or something. Speaking of autopilot, did you see that article in the paper about those new cars they're testing? The ones that drive themselves? It just boggles the mind. I remember when I bought my first car, a '68 Chevy Impala. Beautiful machine. Powder blue. I had to learn how to drive a stick shift on the hills in San Francisco. Talk about nerve-wracking! My older brother taught me. He was a mechanic in the Navy, stationed out in San Diego for a while. He used to tell these stories about the aircraft carriers... massive things, like floating cities. He said they had a dining hall that served thousands of meals a day. Imagine the leftovers they must have had! They probably just tossed it all overboard for the seagulls. Seagulls will eat anything, you know. I was at the pier once, eating a hot dog, and one swooped right down and snatched it out of the bun. Just incredible. \n\nBut yes, to answer your question... once I start eating those leftovers, I just can't seem to make myself stop, even when I know I should.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I... I really don't know if that's it. Sadness or emptiness... I mean, I miss Mary, of course I do, but I don't know if the eating is about that. It's hard to say what's going on in my head when it happens. 

You know, speaking of heads, I had to get a new hat last week. A fedora, just like the one my grandfather used to wear. He was a haberdasher back in Chicago in the twenties. Sold all kinds of hats—bowlers, boaters, panamas. He always said a man wasn't fully dressed without a good hat. He used to take the train into the city every morning. The L train, they called it. I rode it once when I was a boy, visiting for the summer. The noise was deafening, just clattering over the tracks, sparks flying everywhere. We went to Wrigley Field to see the Cubs play. I can't remember who they were playing... maybe the Cardinals? I just remember the smell of roasted peanuts and stale beer. And the ivy on the outfield walls. They don't make stadiums like that anymore. Everything now is just concrete and glass, no character. 

But anyway, what were you asking? Oh, right, the sadness. I just... I don't know. I haven't really thought about it like that. I just find myself in front of the fridge, you know?
